0	\section{Introduction}
1	
2	Hybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect nearly disjoint sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, BCa CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by gaining and losing accuracy on different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Our results show that architectures scoring identically in aggregate may nonetheless differ on 15--45\% of individual predictions, meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.
3	
4	This tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.
5	
6	Most evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate accuracy, which can mask per-example behavioral diversity. Prior work on predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} a

_source line 3406 · 2026-05-22T17:46:51.649881+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA at 1.3B, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, consistent with configuration-specific pathways rather than uniform rescue (dependent on GDN inclusion; does not replicate on other benchmarks). Aggregate benchmark equivalence thus masks per-example divergence: each backbone exhibits configuration-specific response patterns invisible to standard evaluation. Within each architecture, pure-model confidence partially predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74), though disruption thresholds vary 47\% across architectures (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
4	
5	For practitioners, these results motivate per-example audits within the scope of controlled hybrid linear recurrent architectures studied here: the ${\sim}90\%$ non-overlap means architectures interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint examples. A majority vote across three architectures fails to consistently outperform the best individual model (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\,pp; gains on other benchmarks $< 0.2$\,pp) despite 15--45\% disagreement, because roughly half of disagreement examples have only one correct architecture (Appendix~\ref{app:complementarity}). Oracle accuracy reveals $+7$--$14$\,pp of untapped complementarity, but accessing it requires selection methods beyond naive ensembling \citep{wortsman2022model}. Rescue-threshold routing extracts 36\% of oracle complementarity on LAMBADA (+5.3\,pp), demonstrating that confidence-informed selection can partially access this gap, though gains are task-dependent (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
6

22	\maketitle
23	
24	\begin{abstract}
25	Adding attention to linear recurrent architectures is associated with per-example changes on almost entirely different examples across three controlled hybrid linear recurrent designs: pairwise overlap is at most 10\%. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects and find that this predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention dose. Aggregate trends do not reach statistical significance ($n = 5$; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$), yet per-example overlap saturates at 4\% attention and remains flat across all four benchmarks. Naive majority vote fails to exploit this complementarity, suggesting that configurations interchangeable by aggregate metrics carry hidden consequences for per-example reliability.
26	\end{abstract}

_source line 3410 · 2026-05-22T17:46:57.925284+00:00_

42	\subsection{Statistical Methods}\label{ssec:statistical_methods}
43	
44	All statistical tests are two-tailed unless otherwise noted.
45	
46	\paragraph{Permutation test.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, we use an exact permutation test for $\rho$. All $5! = 120$ permutations of the attention ratio ranks are enumerated, and the two-tailed $p$-value is the fraction of permutations yielding $|\rho| \geq |\rho_{\text{obs}}|$.
47	
48	\paragraph{Power considerations.} With $n = 5$ ordinal levels, only $\rho = \pm 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ (exact $p = .017$); the test has no power to detect strong but imperfect monotonic trends ($|\rho| = 0.9$ yields $p = .083$). This binary resolution means that a single rank swap suffices to shift the verdict from significant to non-significant.
49	
50	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. With only $n = 3$ architectures, the resampling space is limited (at most 27 unique resamples before considering ratio permutations), so these intervals should be interpreted as heuristic indicators of effect direction rather than intervals with formal coverage guarantees. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. These intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.
51	
52	\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Six benchmarks were designated confirmatory and four exploratory prior to analysis (Appendix~A); all share the 10-benchmark correction to ensure conservative inference.
53	
54	\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross

33	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
34	
35	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, we verify below that it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). This binarization discards confidence magnitude (a transition from 0.51 to 0.49 is treated identically to one from 0.99 to 0.01), so we supplement it with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).
36	
37	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.104$ for helped, $\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\max} = \min(|A|,|B|)/\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed over

1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Hybrid Recurrent Architectures.}
4	The design space for combining attention with recurrence spans layer-level interleaving \citep[Jamba;][]{lieber2024jamba}, local attention pairing \citep[Griffin, RecurrentGemma;][]{de2024griffin,botev2024recurrentgemma}, matrix-valued recurrence \citep[RWKV-6;][]{peng2024eagle}, within-head mixing \citep[Hymba;][]{dong2024hymba}, and unbounded-context hybrid SSMs \citep[Samba;][]{ren2024samba}. H3 \citep{fu2023hungry} and Hyena \citep{poli2023hyena} establish early design points in the hybrid space with gated convolutions and subquadratic operators. Mamba-2 \citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a duality between structured state spaces and attention; Mamba-3 \citep{lahoti2026mamba3} extends the design further, while \citet{ali2025hidden} reveal implicit attention within Mamba. Comparative evaluations confirm that hybrids outperform pure Transformers and SSMs at scale \citep{waleffe2024empirical}, and that even one attention layer enables content-based routing \citep{basu2026routing}. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide the controlled model zoo we use, holding training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio.
5	
6	\paragraph{Linear Recurrent Models.}
7	Our three recurrent backbones represent distinct gating strategies within the linear recurrence family: RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules. These descend from S4 \citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, respectively, and connect to linear attention \citep{katharopoulos2020transformers} through the state-space--attention duality of Mamba-2.
8	
9	\paragraph{Underspecification and Predictive Multiplicity.}
10	\citet{damour2022underspecification} formalized underspecification: eq

22	\section{Convergence Detail}\label{app:convergence_detail}
23	
24	\begin{figure}[t]
25	\centering
26	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}
27	\caption{Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category.}
28	\label{fig:task_specificity}
29	\end{figure}
30	
31	Beyond LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$), two benchmarks show moderate negative correlations that do not reach significance: ARC-Easy and MMLU (both $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$). Both are classified as ``Mixed'' tasks combining retrieval with reasoning demands. The sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}) shows that MMLU's correlation reverses upon GDN exclusion ($\rho = +0.6$), indicating dependence on a single architecture; ARC-Easy's correlation is more stable. COPA ($n = 100$; supplementary) shows $\rho = -0.67$ with a large $\sigma$ ratio (5.57), consistent with LAMBADA's pattern but unreliable due to small sample size.

28	\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}
29	
30	To move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270; comprehension) and WinoGrande (1{,}267; reasoning, two-choice) appear in Appendix~\ref{app:boolq} and \ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\% attention), which maximizes the contrast between recurrent and hybrid modes; \S\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). We classify each example into one of three categories:
31	
32	\begin{itemize}
33	    \item \textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.
34	    \item \textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.
35	    \item \textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).
36	\end{itemize}
37	
38	The net effect for each architecture is $|\text{helped}| - |\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.
39	
40	To measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \cap B| / |A \cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges. On $K$-choice tasks, bi
…[truncated 314 chars]

765	 & RetNet--HGRN2 & .087 & .115 \\
766	 & RetNet--GLA & .112 & .110 \\
767	 & RetNet--DeltaNet & .138 & .123 \\
768	 & HGRN2--GLA & .098 & .087 \\
769	 & HGRN2--DeltaNet & .109 & .088 \\
770	 & GLA--DeltaNet & .129 & .098 \\
771	 & \textit{Mean} & \textit{.108} & \textit{.105} \\
772	\bottomrule
773	\end{tabular}
774	\end{table}
775	
776	All pairwise Jaccard values remain $\leq 0.14$, consistent with the three-architecture results at 1.3B (Table~\ref{tab:per_example}). The seven new pairs involving GLA and DeltaNet show no systematic difference from the original three pairs (Mann-Whitney $p > .3$ for both helped and hurt), indicating that the low-overlap pattern is not specific to the original architecture triple.
777

_source line 3522 · 2026-05-22T18:56:02.709173+00:00_

9	\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}
10	
11	We evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy was fixed prior to analysis following established distinctions in the hybrid architecture literature \citep{arora2024zoology} (see Appendix~A for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.
12	
13	\begin{itemize}
14	    \item \textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.
15	    \item \textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.
16	    \item \textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) serves as a supplementary reasoning benchmark; its small sample size renders $\alpha$ estimation unreliable (CI width 0.13--0.18; Appendix~\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).
17	    \item \textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	All evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.

7	\caption{Cross-architecture convergence across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\sigma$ values reported $\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$. \textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\rho = -1.0$.}
8	\label{tab:convergence}
9	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}
10	\toprule
11	Benchmark & Category & $\rho$ & $p$ & $\sigma_{\text{pure}}$ & $\sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$ & Ratio \\
12	\midrule
13	\textbf{LAMBADA$^{\ddagger}$} & \textbf{Retrieval} & $\mathbf{-1.0}$ & \textbf{.017} & \textbf{2.41} & \textbf{0.49} & \textbf{4.87} \\
14	\quad\emph{(excl.\ GDN)} & & $-0.7$ & .233 & --- & --- & --- \\
15	BoolQ & Comprehension & $0.2$ & .783 & 1.63 & 2.36 & 0.69 \\
16	ARC-C & Reasoning & $0.6$ & .350 & 0.96 & 1.32 & 0.72 \\
17	HellaSwag & Reasoning & $0.4$ & .517 & 1.17 & 1.22 & 0.96 \\
18	PIQA & Reasoning & $-0.5$ & .450 & 0.54 & 0.34 & 1.59 \\
19	WinoGrande & Reasoning & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.67 & 0.78 & 0.86 \\
20	ARC-E & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 1.00 & 0.13 & 7.70 \\
21	MMLU & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 0.92 & 0.41 & 2.27 \\

23	XStoryCloze & Mixed & $-0.6$ & .350 & 0.82 & 0.55 & 1.49 \\
24	\midrule

15

700	\section{Complementarity Decomposition}\label{app:complementarity}
701	
702	We decompose the three-architecture vote on each example into four categories: unanimous correct (3/3), majority correct (2/3), minority correct (1/3), and unanimous wrong (0/3). This decomposition explains the structural failure of majority vote despite substantial per-example complementarity.
703	
704	Table~\ref{tab:complementarity} reports the decomposition at h3-1 (25\% attention). Majority vote benefits only from 2/3 examples (where the correct answer holds a majority) but is actively harmed by 1/3 examples (where the correct architecture is outvoted). On LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, the MV exploitation ratio---the fraction of complementary examples where MV selects correctly---hovers near 51\%, indicating a near-even split between MV-exploitable and MV-hostile disagreements. The oracle, by contrast, exploits both categories, explaining the large oracle--MV gap ($+7$--$14$\,pp).
705	
706	\begin{table}[ht]
707	\centering
708	\small
709	\caption{Complementarity decomposition at 1.3B h3-1. Fractions of total examples in each vote category. MV expl.: fraction of complementary examples where majority vote is correct.}
710	\label{tab:complementarity}
711	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccr@{}}
712	\toprule
713	Benchmark & 3/3 & 2/3 & 1/3 & 0/3 & MV expl. \\
714	\midrule
715	LAMBADA & .361 & .151 & .145 & .343 & 51.1\% \\
716	HellaSwag & .519 & .078 & .073 & .331 & 51.6\% \\
717	ARC-Easy & .603 & .107 & .103 & .187 & 50.9\% \\
718	BoolQ & .376 & .257 & .197 & .170 & 56.6\% \\
719	\bottomrule
720	\end{tabular}
721	\end{table}
722	
723	This near-50\% exploitation ratio is structurally invariant across attention ratios: on LAMBADA, the ratio ranges from 51.1\% to 54.4\% across all five configurations; on HellaSwag, from 50.3\% to 51.6\%. Attention changes neither the amount nor the structure of cross-architecture complementarity.

39	\section*{Limitations}
40	
41	Our analysis has several limitations.
42	
43	\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\times$ within- vs.\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence. All per-example claims are conditioned on this limitation. With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable $p$-value is 0.017, contributing to LAMBADA's Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$ not reaching conventional significance. As a partial calibration, we compare within-seed Jaccard from Pythia 70M PolyPythias \citep{vanderwal2025polypythias} against our cross-architecture values (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}); within-seed overlap is 2.7--4.3$\times$ higher, consistent with, but not definitive evidence for, architecture-driven divergence. Future work should conduct multi-seed training on matched architectures to fully isolate architectural from seed-specific effects.
44	
45	\paragraph{Coverage and generalization.} We examine three primary architectures from one model zoo on four main benchmarks (two supplementary); two additional architectures (GLA and DeltaNet) confirm the pattern at 340M (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). The controlled design isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families. We compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B). Our analysis does not cover Mamba, RWKV, or generation tasks; generalization to these architectures and task types remains open.
46	
47	\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics may reveal different multiplicity patterns.
48

18	\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}
19	
20	\author{Anonymous}
21	
22	\begin{document}
23	\maketitle
24	
25	\begin{abstract}
26	Adding attention to linear recurrent architectures is associated with per-example changes on substantially different examples across three controlled hybrid linear recurrent designs (Jaccard $\approx$ 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline). Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects and find that this predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention dose. Aggregate trends do not reach statistical significance ($n = 5$; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$), yet per-example overlap saturates at 4\% attention and remains flat across all four benchmarks. Naive majority vote fails to exploit this complementarity, suggesting that configurations interchangeable by aggregate metrics carry hidden consequences for per-example reliability.
27	\end{abstract}
28

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Hybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect largely non-overlapping sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, BCa CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by gaining and losing accuracy on different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Across the five architectures examined here, architectures scoring identically in aggregate may nonetheless differ on 15--45\% of individual predictions, meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.
4	
5	This tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.
6	
7	Most evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate accuracy, which can mask per-example behavioral diversity. Prior work on predictive multiplici

_source line 3632 · 2026-05-22T21:08:00.985761+00:00_

78	Cross-scale analysis on all four benchmarks reveals that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is scale-robust: pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M vs.\ $0.09$--$0.095$ at 1.3B), and configuration-specific helped/hurt profiles persist. While $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp), this inflation does not correlate with accuracy loss across benchmarks (HellaSwag shows the largest accuracy gap yet one of the smallest $\alpha$ increases), suggesting capacity-related but not accuracy-driven modulation. At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity (which architectures affect which examples) reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}).
79	
80	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
81	
82	Removing GDN weakens LAMBADA convergence from $\rho = -1.0$ to $\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$) and reverses MMLU convergence from $\rho = -0.7$ to $\rho = +0.6$, indicating that the MMLU trend was entirely driven by GDN's 16-head configuration. GDN's h3-1 uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 in all other configurations (Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The 16-vs-32 head asymmetry could contribute to GDN's faster convergence by limiting attention capacity, though disentangling this from architectural differences would require matched-head experiments not available in the zoo. The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\sigma \leq 0.008$ while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108. The aggregate convergence result is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. An extended analysis including two additional architectures (GLA and DeltaNet) confirms that cross-architecture Jaccard remains uniformly $\leq 0.14$

{
  "command": "python3 << 'PYEOF'\nimport matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\n\narchs = ['GDN', 'RetNet', 'HGRN2', 'GLA', 'DeltaNet']\nn = len(archs)\n\n# LAMBADA data: Jh (upper triangle), Ju (lower triangle)\nlambada_jh = {\n    (0,1): .128, (0,2): .123, (0,3): .124, (0,4): .115,\n    (1,2): .108, (1,3): .123, (1,4): .115,\n    (2,3): .127, (2,4): .125,\n    (3,4): .134\n}\nlambada_ju = {\n    (0,1): .121, (0,2): .083, (0,3): .100, (0,4): .100,\n    (1,2): .083, (1,3): .107, (1,4): .096,\n    (2,3): .086, (2,4): .090,\n    (3,4): .081\n}\n\n# ARC-Easy data\narce_jh = {\n    (0,1): .089, (0,2): .122, (0,3): .114, (0,4): .084,\n    (1,2): .087, (1,3): .112, (1,4): .138,\n    (2,3): .098, (2,4): .109,\n    (3,4): .129\n}\narce_ju = {\n    (0,1): .114, (0,2): .105, (0,3): .106, (0,4): .102,\n    (1,2): .115, (1,3): .110, (1,4): .123,\n    (2,3): .087, (2,4): .088,\n    (3,4): .098\n}\n\ndef build_matrix(jh, ju, n):\n    mat = np.full((n, n), np.nan)\n    for (i, j), v in jh.items():\n        mat[i, j] = v  # upper triangle = helped\n    for (i, j), v in ju.items():\n        mat[j, i] = v  # lower triangle = hurt\n    return mat\n\nfig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(10, 4.5))\n\nfor ax, data_jh, data_ju, title in [\n    (axes[0], lambada_jh, lambada_ju, 'LAMBADA'),\n    (axes[1], arce_jh, arce_ju, 'ARC-Easy')\n]:\n    mat = build_matrix(data_jh, data_ju, n)\n    \n    # Create masked array for display\n    mask = np.eye(n, dtype=bool)\n    masked = np.ma.array(mat, mask=mask)\n    \n    im = ax.imshow(masked, cmap='YlGnBu', vmin=0, vmax=0.20, aspect='equal')\n    \n    # Annotate cells\n    for i in range(n):\n        for j in range(n):\n            if i == j:\n                ax.add_patch(plt.Rectangle((j-0.5, i-0.5), 1, 1, fill=True, color='0.85', zorder=2))\n            elif not np.isnan(mat[i, j]):\n                ax.text(j, i, f'{mat[i,j]:.2f}', ha='center', va='center', fontsize=9,\n                       color='white' if mat[i,j] > 0.12 else 'black')\n    \n    ax.set_xticks(range(n))\n    ax.set_yticks(range(n))\n    ax.set_xticklabels(archs, fontsize=9, rotation=45, ha='right')\n    ax.set_yticklabels(archs, fontsize=9)\n    ax.set_title(title, fontsize=11, fontweight='bold')\n    \n    # Add triangle labels\n    ax.text(n-1.3, 0.3, r'$J_h$', fontsize=10, fontstyle='italic', color='0.3')\n    ax.text(0.3, n-1.3, r'$J_u$', fontsize=10, fontstyle='italic', color='0.3')\n\n# Colorbar\ncbar = fig.colorbar(im, ax=axes, shrink=0.8, pad=0.02)\ncbar.set_label('Jaccard similarity', fontsize=10)\n\nfig.suptitle('')\nplt.tight_layout()\nplt.savefig('/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf',\n            bbox_inches='tight', dpi=300)\nplt.savefig('/tmp/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.png', bbox_inches='tight', dpi=150)\nprint(\"DONE\")\nPYEOF",
  "description": "Generate 5-arch Jaccard heatmap locally from table data",
  "timeout": "30000"
}

(0,4): .102,\n    (1,2): .115, (1,3): .110, (1,4): .123,\n    (2,3): .087, (2,4): .088,\n    (3,4): .098\n}\n\ndef build_matrix(jh, ju, n):\n    mat = np.full((n, n), np.nan)\n    for (i, j), v in jh.items():\n        mat[i, j] = v\n    for (i, j), v in ju.items():\n        mat[j, i] = v\n    return mat\n\nfig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(10, 4.5))\n\nfor ax, data_jh, data_ju, title in [\n    (axes[0], lambada_jh, lambada_ju, 'LAMBADA'),\n    (axes[1], arce_jh, arce_ju, 'ARC-Easy')\n]:\n    mat = build_matrix(data_jh, data_ju, n)\n    mask = np.eye(n, dtype=bool)\n    masked = np.ma.array(mat, mask=mask)\n    \n    im = ax.imshow(masked, cmap='YlGnBu', vmin=0, vmax=0.20, aspect='equal')\n    \n    for i in range(n):\n        for j in range(n):\n            if i == j:\n                ax.add_patch(plt.Rectangle((j-0.5, i-0.5), 1, 1, fill=True, color='0.85', zorder=2))\n            elif not np.isnan(mat[i, j]):\n                ax.text(j, i, f'{mat[i,j]:.2f}', ha='center', va='center', fontsize=9,\n                       color='white' if mat[i,j] > 0.12 else 'black')\n    \n    ax.set_xticks(range(n))\n    ax.set_yticks(range(n))\n    ax.set_xticklabels(archs, fontsize=9, rotation=45, ha='right')\n    ax.set_yticklabels(archs, fontsize=9)\n    ax.set_title(title, fontsize=11, fontweight='bold')\n    ax.text(n-1.3, 0.3, r'$J_h$', fontsize=10, fontstyle='italic', color='0.3')\n    ax.text(0.3, n-1.3, r'$J_u$', fontsize=10, fontstyle='italic', color='0.3')\n\ncbar = fig.colorbar(im, ax=axes, shrink=0.8, pad=0.02)\ncbar.set_label('Jaccard similarity', fontsize=10)\n\nplt.tight_layout()\nplt.savefig('/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf',\n            bbox_inches='tight', dpi=300)\nplt.savefig('/tmp/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.png', bbox_inches='tight', dpi=150)\nprint(\"DONE\")\nPYEOF",
  "description": "Generate 5-arch Jaccard heatmap",
  "timeout": "30000"
}

[Director 指令] [评估请求] Related Work 新鲜度检查：

论文目前 Related Work 引用了 2026 年最新工作（Mamba-3, Gorbett et al., Haghighat et al.）。请评估：

1. 2025 年下半年到 2026 年 5 月，predictive multiplicity / model disagreement / underspecification / per-example analysis 领域有没有新的重要工作遗漏？
2. Hybrid linear recurrent architecture（GDN/RetNet/HGRN2/Mamba 等）领域有没有新 benchmark 或新发现与我们的 claim 直接相关？
3. 当前 Related Work 的组织结构和 positioning 是否最优——是否清楚地将我们的工作与 (a) predictive multiplicity theory, (b) underspecification empirics, (c) hybrid architecture design 三个 stream 区分开？

如果发现遗漏的重要工作，给出引用建议。如果认为当前已足够，说"Related Work 覆盖充分"即可。

_source line 3724 · 2026-05-22T22:22:03.761896+00:00_

1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Hybrid Recurrent Architectures.}
4	The design space for combining attention with recurrence spans layer-level interleaving \citep[Jamba;][]{lieber2024jamba}, local attention pairing \citep[Griffin, RecurrentGemma;][]{de2024griffin,botev2024recurrentgemma}, matrix-valued recurrence \citep[RWKV-6;][]{peng2024eagle}, within-head mixing \citep[Hymba;][]{dong2024hymba}, and unbounded-context hybrid SSMs \citep[Samba;][]{ren2024samba}. H3 \citep{fu2023hungry} and Hyena \citep{poli2023hyena} establish early design points in the hybrid space with gated convolutions and subquadratic operators. Mamba-2 \citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a duality between structured state spaces and attention; Mamba-3 \citep{lahoti2026mamba3} extends the design further, while \citet{ali2025hidden} reveal implicit attention within Mamba. Comparative evaluations confirm that hybrids outperform pure Transformers and SSMs at scale \citep{waleffe2024empirical}, and that even one attention layer enables content-based routing \citep{basu2026routing}. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide the controlled model zoo we use, holding training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio.
5	
6	\paragraph{Linear Recurrent Models.}
7	Our three recurrent backbones represent distinct gating strategies within the linear recurrence family: RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules. These descend from S4 \citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, respectively, and connect to linear attention \citep{katharopoulos2020transformers} through the state-space--attention duality of Mamba-2.
8	
9	\paragraph{Underspecification and Predictive Multiplicity.}
10	\citet{damour2022underspecification} formalized underspecification: eq
…[truncated 2765 chars]

_source line 3732 · 2026-05-22T22:28:08.611325+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA at 1.3B, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, consistent with configuration-specific pathways rather than uniform rescue (dependent on GDN inclusion; does not replicate on other benchmarks). Across the architectures studied here, aggregate benchmark equivalence masks per-example divergence: each backbone exhibits configuration-specific response patterns invisible to standard evaluation. Within each architecture, pure-model confidence partially predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74), though disruption thresholds vary 47\% across architectures (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
4	
5	For practitioners, these results motivate per-example audits within the scope of controlled hybrid linear recurrent architectures studied here: the ${\sim}90\%$ non-overlap means architectures interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect largely non-overlapping examples. A majority vote across three architectures fails to consistently outperform the best individual model (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\,pp; gains on other benchmarks $< 0.2$\,pp) despite 15--45\% disagreement, because roughly half of disagreement examples have only one correct architecture (Appendix~\ref{app:complementarity}). Oracle accuracy reveals $+7$--$14$\,pp of untapped complementarity, but accessing it requires selection methods beyond naive ensembling \citep{wortsman2022model}. Rescue-threshold routing extracts 36\% of oracle complementarity on LAMBADA (+5.3\,pp), demonstrating that confidence-informed selection can partially access this gap, though gains are task-dependent (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
6

_source line 3734 · 2026-05-22T22:28:09.113992+00:00_

25	\begin{abstract}
26	Adding attention to linear recurrent architectures is associated with per-example changes on substantially different examples across three controlled hybrid linear recurrent designs (Jaccard $\approx$ 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline); an extended validation across five architectures and ten pairwise comparisons confirms this pattern (all $p \leq .002$). Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects and find that this predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention dose. Aggregate trends do not reach statistical significance ($n = 5$; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$), yet per-example overlap saturates at 4\% attention and remains flat across all four benchmarks. Naive majority vote fails to exploit this complementarity, suggesting that configurations interchangeable by aggregate metrics carry hidden consequences for per-example reliability.
27	\end{abstract}

_source line 3736 · 2026-05-22T22:28:09.516393+00:00_

39	\section*{Limitations}
40	
41	Our analysis has several limitations.
42	
43	\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\times$ within- vs.\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence. All per-example claims are conditioned on this limitation. With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable $p$-value is 0.017, contributing to LAMBADA's Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$ not reaching conventional significance. As a partial calibration, we compare within-seed Jaccard from Pythia 70M PolyPythias \citep{vanderwal2025polypythias} against our cross-architecture values (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}); within-seed overlap is 2.7--4.3$\times$ higher, consistent with, but not definitive evidence for, architecture-driven divergence. Future work should conduct multi-seed training on matched architectures to fully isolate architectural from seed-specific effects.
44	
45	\paragraph{Coverage and generalization.} We examine three primary architectures from one model zoo on four main benchmarks (two supplementary); two additional architectures (GLA and DeltaNet) confirm the pattern at 340M (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). The controlled design isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families. We compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B). Our analysis does not cover Mamba, RWKV, or generation tasks; generalization to these architectures and task types remains open.
46	
47	\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics may reveal different multiplicity patterns.
48
